<hgroup><h1>Standardized multi-omics of Earth’s microbiomes reveals microbial and metabolite diversity</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Shaffer%20JP"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Justin P Shaffer</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Justin P Shaffer</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Pediatrics, School of Medicine, University of California San Diego, La Jolla, CA USA </div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Shaffer%20JP"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Justin P Shaffer</span></a>
</div>
</div>

</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="Par1">Despite advances in sequencing, lack of standardization makes comparisons across studies challenging and hampers insights into the structure and function of microbial communities across multiple habitats on a planetary scale. Here we present a multi-omics analysis of a diverse set of 880 microbial community samples collected for the Earth Microbiome Project. We include amplicon (16S, 18S, ITS) and shotgun metagenomic sequence data, and untargeted metabolomics data (liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry and gas chromatography mass spectrometry). We used standardized protocols and analytical methods to characterize microbial communities, focusing on relationships and co-occurrences of microbially related metabolites and microbial taxa across environments, thus allowing us to explore diversity at extraordinary scale. In addition to a reference database for metagenomic and metabolomic data, we provide a framework for incorporating additional studies, enabling the expansion of existing knowledge in the form of an evolving community resource. We demonstrate the utility of this database by testing the hypothesis that every microbe and metabolite is everywhere but the environment selects. Our results show that metabolite diversity exhibits turnover and nestedness related to both microbial communities and the environment, whereas the relative abundances of microbially related metabolites vary and co-occur with specific microbial consortia in a habitat-specific manner. We additionally show the power of certain chemistry, in particular terpenoids, in distinguishing Earth’s environments (for example, terrestrial plant surfaces and soils, freshwater and marine animal stool), as well as that of certain microbes including <em>Conexibacter woesei</em> (terrestrial soils), <em>Haloquadratum walsbyi</em> (marine deposits) and <em>Pantoea dispersa</em> (terrestrial plant detritus). This Resource provides insight into the taxa and metabolites within microbial communities from diverse habitats across Earth, informing both microbial and chemical ecology, and provides a foundation and methods for multi-omics microbiome studies of hosts and the environment.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Subject terms:</strong> Microbial ecology, Chemical ecology, Metabolomics, Next-generation sequencing, Cheminformatics</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="Abs2"><hr class="headless">
<p id="Par2">This Resource combines amplicon sequencing, shotgun metagenomic sequencing and untargeted metabolomics to provide a global view of microbial–metabolite associations across Earth’s environments.</p></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Main</h2>
<p id="Par3">A major goal in microbial ecology is to understand structure in microbial communities, how this is related to microbial taxonomic, phylogenetic and functional composition, and how those relationships vary across space and time. As any single study is not able to sample all environments repeatedly to allow for such inferences, fostering the use of standardized methods that permit meta-analysis across distinct studies is of utmost importance<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>–<a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a></sup>. Initial efforts focused on standardized protocols for 16S ribosomal RNA (rRNA) sequencing of bacterial/archaeal communities provided insight into how communities structure in the environment, supporting strong axes of separation of microbes along gradients of host association and salinity<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>,<a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">5</a></sup>. More recent efforts focused on shotgun metagenomics data<sup><a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>–<a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a></sup> have begun to provide additional insight regarding functional potential across environments<sup><a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>–<a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a></sup>, and the current state-of-the-art methods employ multi-omics approaches including metagenomics, transcriptomics, proteomics and/or metabolomics<sup><a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a>–<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par4">Microbes produce diverse secondary metabolites that perform vital functions from communication to defence<sup><a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a>–<a href="#CR27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR27">27</a></sup> and can benefit human health and environmental sustainability<sup><a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">28</a>–<a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">34</a></sup>. Whereas metagenome mining and transcriptomics are powerful ways to characterize function in microbial communities<sup><a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>,<a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a>,<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a></sup>, a more powerful approach to understanding functional diversity is to generate chemical evidence that confirms the presence of metabolites<sup><a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>–<a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">21</a></sup> and accurately describes their distribution across Earth. Here we present an approach that directly assesses the presence and relative abundance of metabolites, and provides an accurate description of metabolite profiles in microbial communities across Earth’s environments. Although several studies have previously employed tandem metagenomics and metabolomics<sup><a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>,<a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a>,<a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>–<a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a></sup>, many employed relatively limited technical methods or profiled a relatively small number of classes of metabolites<sup><a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a>,<a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>,<a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a></sup>, preventing comparison across studies that could expand our understanding. Further, several previous studies are limited in scope to a single environment or habitat<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>,<a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a>,<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>,<a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>–<a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a></sup>. Our work goes substantially beyond what has been reported previously regarding multi-omics analysis of microbial communities using metagenomics and metabolomics, by including multiple ecosystems. The approach we apply complements metagenomics with a direct survey of secondary metabolites using untargeted metabolomics.</p>
<p id="Par5">Liquid chromatography with untargeted tandem mass spectrometry (LC–MS/MS) is a versatile method that detects tens of thousands of metabolites in biological samples<sup><a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a></sup>. Although LC–MS/MS metabolomics has historically suffered from low metabolite annotation rates when applied to non-model organisms, recent computational advances can systematically assign chemical classes to metabolites using their fragmentation spectra<sup><a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a></sup>. Untargeted mass-spectrometry-based metabolomics provides the relative abundance (that is, intensity) of each metabolite detected across samples rather than just counts of unique structures (that is, presence/absence data), and thus provides a direct readout of the surveyed environment, complementing a purely genomics-based approach. Although there is a clear need to use untargeted metabolomics to quantify the metabolic activities of microbiota, the approach has been limited by the challenge of distinguishing the secondary metabolites produced exclusively by microbes from other compounds detected in the environment (for example, those produced by multicellular hosts). To resolve this bottleneck, we devised a computational method for recognizing and annotating putative secondary metabolites of microbial origin from fragmentation spectra (see Online Methods).</p>
<p id="Par6">We used this methodology to quantify microbial secondary metabolites from diverse microbial communities from the Earth Microbiome Project (EMP, <a href="http://earthmicrobiome.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://earthmicrobiome.org</a>). The EMP was founded in 2010 to sample Earth’s microbial communities at unprecedented scale, in part to advance our understanding of biogeographic processes that shape community structure. To avoid confusion with terminology, we define ‘microbial community’ as consisting of members of the domains Bacteria and Archaea. To build on the first analysis of the EMP archive focused on profiling bacterial and archaeal 16S rRNA<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>, we crowd-sourced a previously undescribed set of roughly 900 samples from the scientific community specifically for multi-omics analysis. We expanded the scalable framework of the EMP to include standardized methods for shotgun metagenomic sequencing and untargeted metabolomics for cataloguing microbiota globally. As a result, we provide a rich resource for addressing outstanding questions and to serve as a benchmark for acquiring additional data. To provide an example for using this resource, we present a multi-omics analysis of this undescribed sample set, tracking not just individual sequences but also genomes and metabolites. Our analysis includes diverse studies with sample types classified using an updated and standardized environmental ontology, describes large-scale ecological patterns and explores important questions in microbial ecology.</p>
<p id="Par7">Specifically, we explore the hypothesis that ‘everything is everywhere but the environment selects’<sup><a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">42</a>–<a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">46</a></sup>. We predict that although most major classes of metabolites have cosmopolitan distributions<sup><a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a></sup>, their relative abundances will vary strongly among different environments. Therefore, whereas the presence/absence of metabolites alone may show profiles that are relatively uniform across samples, their relative abundances will provide great power in distinguishing among habitats. We predict that similar to microbes<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>, metabolites will exhibit both turnover and nestedness across habitats. Furthermore, we expect variation in metabolite profiles among environments to be in part driven by variation in microbial community composition. Therefore, we explore the hypothesis that metabolite alpha- and beta-diversity will be strongly correlated with microbial diversity. We anticipate strong positive relationships between microbial diversity and metabolite diversity, but that environmental similarity based on microbial composition may be distinct from that based on metabolite composition. We suspect that this is in part due to deterministic processes unique to microbial community assembly and similarity in metabolite profiles across the microbial phylogeny<sup><a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">47</a>–<a href="#CR49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR49">49</a></sup>. Regardless, if profiles for metabolites and microbes are habitat-specific, we predict that certain members can be used to classify samples among environments. We also predict that metabolites will co-occur with specific microbial taxa such that metabolite–microbe pairs can be described as features in the environment that define specific habitats.</p></section><section id="Sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="Sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">A resource for multi-omics in microbial ecological research</h3>
<p id="Par8">Here we generated data for 880 environmental samples that span 19 major environments contributed by 34 principal investigators as part of the Earth Microbiome Project 500 (EMP500). The EMP500 is a previously unreported sample set for multi-omics protocol development and data exploration (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">1</a>). To normalize sample collection for this and future studies, we updated and followed the existing Earth Microbiome Project (EMP) sample submission guide (<a href="https://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/emp500-sample-submission-guide/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/emp500-sample-submission-guide/</a>)<sup><a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">50</a></sup>, which we highlight here to encourage its use. In parallel, we followed standardized protocols for sample collection, sample tracking, sample metadata curation, sample shipping and data release, which are also detailed on the EMP website (<a href="https://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/</a>) and described here (see Online Methods). Importantly, we updated the previous EMP Metadata Guide to accommodate the EMP500 sampling design as well as updates to other standardized ontologies (see Online Methods), including the Earth Microbiome Project Ontology (EMPO). EMPO classifies microbial environments (level 4) on the basis of host association (level 1), salinity (level 2), host kingdom (if host-associated) or phase (if free-living) (level 3) (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1a</a>). EMPO now recognizes an important split within host-associated samples representing saline and non-saline environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1a</a>) not detected in the EMP’s previous analysis of 16S rRNA from a separate set of &lt;23,000 samples<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1. Environment type and provenance of samples.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig1_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/25871000124f/41564_2022_1266_Fig1_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e2173" height="255" width="720" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, Distribution of samples (<em>n</em> = 880) among the Earth Microbiome Project Ontology (EMPO version 2) categories. EMPO recognizes strong axes of variation in microbial communities, and thus organizes all microbial environments (level 4) on the basis of host association (level 1), salinity (level 2), host taxon (for host-associated) or phase (free-living) (level 3). For EMPO 3 and EMPO 4: n-s, non-saline; s, saline. Colours indicate environments. Numbers indicate sample counts for each environment. Made with JSFiddle. <strong>b</strong>, Geographic distribution of samples with points coloured by EMPO 4. Points are transparent to highlight cases where multiple samples derive from a single location. We note here that our intent was to sample across environments rather than geography, in part because we previously showed that microbial community composition is more influenced by the former rather than the latter, but also to motivate finer-grained geographic exploration as sample analyses decrease in cost. Extensive information about each sample set is described in Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>. Made with Natural Earth.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par9">For the majority of samples, we successfully generated data for bacterial and archaeal 16S rRNA, eukaryotic 18S rRNA, internal transcribed spacer (ITS) 1 of the fungal ITS region, bacterial full-length rRNA operon, shotgun metagenomics and untargeted metabolomics (that is, LC–MS/MS and gas chromatography coupled with mass spectrometry (GC–MS)) (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">2</a>). To foster exploration of this previously unreported dataset, we have made the raw sequence and metabolomics data publicly available through Qiita (<a href="https://qiita.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://qiita.ucsd.edu</a>; study ID: 13114)<sup><a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">51</a></sup> and GNPS (<a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu</a>; MassIVE IDs: <a href="https://massive.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/QueryMSV?id=MSV000083475" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MSV000083475</a>, <a href="https://massive.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/QueryMSV?id=MSV000083743" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MSV000083743</a>)<sup><a href="#CR52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR52">52</a></sup>, respectively. We also provide complete protocols for laboratory and computational workflows for both metagenomics and metabolomics data for use by the broader community (available on GitHub at <a href="https://github.com/biocore/emp/blob/master/methods/methods_release2.md" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://github.com/biocore/emp/blob/master/methods/methods_release2.md</a>). We hope that the dataset and workflows presented here serve as useful tools for others, in addition to providing a framework for launching additional future studies. As an example of the utility of the dataset for addressing important questions in microbial community ecology, we present an analysis of microbially related metabolites and microbe–metabolite co-occurrences across Earth’s environments (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 1. Diagrammatic overview of multi-omics analyses performed using the EMP500 dataset.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig6_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/4637f84b2cf8/41564_2022_1266_Fig6_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e4876" height="483" width="722" alt="Extended Data Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The process begins with data generation for both the microbiome and metabolome, which is then followed by analysis of differential abundance of both microbial taxa and microbially-related metabolites across environments. To begin multi-omics integration, correlations between alpha- and beta-diversity are explored, followed by explicit co-occurrence analysis of metabolite-microbe pairs. The results from analysis of co-occurrence are then combined with those from analysis of differential abundance, to reveal strong patterns of metabolite-microbe turnover across environments. Throughout the diagram, artifacts derived from microbial data are outlined in yellow, those derived from metabolite data are outlined in blue, and those derived from co-occurrence analysis are outlined in green.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolite intensities reveal habitat-specific distributions</h3>
<p id="Par10">In total, we generated untargeted metabolomics data (that is, LC–MS/MS) for 618 of 880 samples (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">2</a>), resulting in 52,496 unique molecular structures, or metabolites, across all samples. We then refined that dataset to include only putative, microbially related metabolites (that is, defined as being produced, modified by, or otherwise associated with a microbe), resulting in 6,588 metabolites across all samples (12.55% of all metabolites). Focusing on this subset, we found that although the presence/absence of major classes of microbially related metabolites is relatively conserved across habitats, their relative intensities (that is, analogous to relative abundances for microbes) reveal specific chemistry that is lacking or enriched in particular environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a> and Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig7" class="usa-link">2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2. Distribution of microbially related secondary metabolite pathways and superclasses among environments.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/9d13f6ac2ae4/41564_2022_1266_Fig2_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e2264" height="687" width="722" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>–<strong>d</strong>, Individual metabolites are represented by their higher-level classifications. Both chemical pathway and chemical superclass annotations are shown on the basis of presence/absence (<strong>a</strong>,<strong>c</strong>) and relative intensities (<strong>b</strong>,<strong>d</strong>) of molecular features, respectively. For superclass annotations in <strong>c</strong> and <strong>d</strong>, we included pathway annotations (when possible) for metabolites where superclass annotations were not available, and colours identify superclasses and pathways.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 2. Relative abundance of microbially-related metabolite pathways, highlighting among-sample variation for each environment.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/36abc3667377/41564_2022_1266_Fig7_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e4899" height="563" width="676" alt="Extended Data Fig. 2"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>These data are shown as a complement to those in Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2b</a> of the main text. We note that as abundance data were not normalized (for example, by using log-ratios as in Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a</a>), caution should be used in interpreting differences among environments. Boxplots are in the style of Tukey, where the center line indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first- and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker 1.5 x the interquartile range (IQR) from its respective hinge. For each panel, <em>n</em> = 618 biologically independent samples, and the number of metabolites per pathway is shown.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par11">Importantly, when considering differences in the relative intensities of all microbially related metabolites, profiles for each habitat were so distinct that we could identify particular metabolites whose abundances were significantly enriched in certain environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a>). For example, metabolites annotated as carbohydrates (that is, excluding glycosides) were enriched in aquatic samples (log fold change (LFC)<sub>Water (non-saline)</sub> = 0.31 ± 1.22, LFC<sub>Water (saline)</sub> = 0.54 ± 1.45) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a</a>). Similarly, sediment, marine plant surface and fungal samples were enriched in polyketides (LFC<sub>Sediment (non-saline)</sub> = 1.69 ± 0.64, LFC<sub>Sediment (saline)</sub> = 1.56 ± 1.11, LFC<sub>Plant surface (saline)</sub> = 1.22 ± 0.35, LFC<sub>Fungus corpus (non-saline)</sub> = 1.68 ± 1.10) and soil, lake sediment and marine plant surface samples were enriched in shikimates and phenylpropanoids (LFC<sub>Sediment (non-saline)</sub> = 1.90 ± 0.69, LFC<sub>Soil (non-saline)</sub> = 1.33 ± 0.65, LFC<sub>Plant surface (saline)</sub> = 1.09 ±0.43) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3. Structural-level associations between microbially related secondary metabolites and specific environments.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig3_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/c59c975157d6/41564_2022_1266_Fig3_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e2416" height="447" width="722" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, Differential abundance of metabolites across environments. For each panel, the <em>y</em> axis represents the natural log-ratio of the intensities of ingroup metabolites divided by the intensities of reference group metabolites (that is, pathway reference: Amino acids and peptides, <em>n</em> = 615; superclass reference: Flavonoids, <em>n</em> = 42). The number of metabolites in each ingroup and the chi-squared statistic from a Kruskal–Wallis (KW) test for differences across environments are shown. For each test, <em>n</em> = 606 samples and <em>P</em> &lt; 2.2 × 10<sup>−16</sup>. Boxplots are Tukey’s, where the centre indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker is 1.5× the interquartile range (IQR) from its hinge. <strong>b</strong>, Relationship between metabolite richness and microbial taxon richness, with significant correlations noted. <em>P</em> values are from two-tailed tests and were adjusted using the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure. <strong>c</strong>, Turnover in composition of metabolites across environments, visualized using RPCA, showing samples separated on the basis of metabolite abundances. Shapes represent samples. Arrows represent metabolites and are coloured by chemical pathway. The direction and magnitude of each arrow corresponds to the correlation between the metabolite’s abundance and the ordination axes. Samples close to arrow heads have strong positive associations, samples at arrow origins have no association, and those beyond arrow origins have strong negative associations. Metabolites are described in Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">4</a>. Metabolites annotated in red and purple were also highly differentially abundant across environments (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a>), and those in purple were also identified as important in co-occurrence analyses (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a>). <strong>d</strong>, Turnover in composition of microbial taxa across environments, visualized using PCoA of weighted UniFrac distances. For <strong>c</strong> and <strong>d</strong>, results from PERMANOVA (999 permutations) for each level of EMPO are shown (all tests had <em>P</em> = 0.001; group sizes for metabolites: <em>k</em><sub>EMPO1</sub> = 2, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO2</sub> = 4, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO3</sub> = 9, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO4</sub> = 18; group sizes for microbial taxa: <em>k</em><sub>EMPO1</sub> = 2, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO2</sub> = 4, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO3</sub> = 9, <em>k</em><sub>EMPO4</sub> = 19). Sample sizes in <strong>a</strong> refer to metabolites, but in all other panels refer to samples.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par12">The total number of distinct metabolites (that is, richness) also varied strongly across environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a>). We note that whereas saline sediments were most rich, the surfaces of terrestrial plants were especially lacking in metabolite diversity (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a>). This contrasted with metabolite diversity in detritus of terrestrial plants, which was also high (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a>).</p>
<p id="Par13">When considering the identity and relative intensity of each metabolite in the analysis of beta-diversity, we observed a separation of samples based on host association and salinity (permutational multivariate analysis of variance (PERMANOVA) for EMPO 2: pseudo-<em>F</em> = 92.66, <em>P</em> = 0.001), and among specific environments (PERMANOVA for EMPO 4: pseudo-<em>F</em> = 48.63, <em>P</em> = 0.001). We also observed specific environments clustering in ordination space and identified certain metabolite features that differentiate all samples (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">4</a>). For the latter, we identified three metabolites also listed among the 10 most differentially abundant metabolites for each environment (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a>): one chalcone associated with the surfaces of terrestrial plants (C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O, ID: 4949), one glycerolipid associated with freshwater (C<sub>28</sub>H<sub>58</sub>O<sub>15</sub>, ID: 14665) and one cholane steroid associated with the distal guts of terrestrial animals (C<sub>24</sub>H<sub>34</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, ID: 25552) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c</a>). As the separation of samples based on metabolite profiles appeared to mirror those based on microbial taxa (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c,d</a>), we additionally explored our shotgun metagenomics data.</p></section><section id="Sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Correlation between metabolite and microbial alpha-diversity</h3>
<p id="Par14">We first explored whether metabolite alpha-diversity was related to microbe alpha-diversity. We found significant positive correlations between metabolite richness and microbial taxon richness across all samples (<em>r</em> = 0.20, <em>P</em> &lt; 0.001), within host-associated samples (<em>r</em> = 0.19, <em>P</em> &lt; 0.01), within free-living samples (<em>r</em> = 0.18, <em>P</em> &lt; 0.05) and for certain environments: Animal proximal gut (saline) (<em>r</em> = 0.73, <em>P</em> &lt; 0.01), Plant detritus (non-saline) (<em>r</em> = 0.74, <em>P</em> &lt; .001), Sediment (non-saline) (<em>r</em> = 0.42, <em>P</em> = 0.05) and Water (saline) (<em>r</em> = 0.57, <em>P</em> = 0.01) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">6</a>). We observed non-significant trends in correlations for Plant surface (non-saline) (<em>r</em> = −0.36, <em>P</em> = 0.2) and Sediment (saline) (<em>r</em> = 0.27, <em>P</em> = 0.1) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">6</a>). Relationships for other environments were weaker (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">6</a>). Sediment samples had the highest alpha-diversity of both microbial taxa and metabolites (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3b</a>). Correlations with metabolite richness were weaker when using Faith’s phylogenetic diversity (PD) and weighted Faith’s PD for microbial taxa (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">6</a>).</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Turnover and nestedness are related to the environment</h3>
<p id="Par15">Next, we examined whether metabolite diversity among environments (that is, beta-diversity) was driven by either turnover (that is, the replacement of features) or nestedness (gain/loss of features leading to differences in richness)<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>,<a href="#CR53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR53">53</a></sup>. We first looked at turnover. We already noted similarity in the clustering of samples by environment between microbially related metabolite and microbial taxon datasets (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c,d</a>). We also observed a strong correlation between sample–sample distances based on metabolites vs microbial taxa (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). Interestingly, we observed a stronger effect of salinity when comparing samples on the basis of microbial taxa vs metabolites (PERMANOVA on salinity: pseudo-<em>F</em> = 40.94 for microbes vs 8.25 for metabolites, <em>P</em> = 0.001 for both tests) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c,d</a>). Furthermore, when focusing on the separation of samples within a single environment such as soil, we observed much more variability between metabolite and microbial taxon datasets (Mantel <em>r</em> = 0.32 for soil vs 0.43 for all environments, <em>P</em> = 0.001 for both tests). This highlights the unique composition among soil samples from distinct locations (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig8" class="usa-link">3</a>), and also the insight that was gained from analysis at different scales (that is, only soils vs all habitats). To assess whether metabolite profiles were more similar to those for microbial taxa vs microbial functions, we annotated our metagenomic reads to profile enzymes. We found the separation of samples based on microbial functions to be unique and largely driven by animal gut samples as compared to separation based on either metabolites or microbial taxa (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">4</a>). However, correlations in sample–sample distances between microbial functional data and other datasets were strong (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>).</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p20"><p>Note the strong relationships between the metabolomics data (that is, LC–MS/MS and GC–MS) and the sequence data from Bacteria and Archaea (that is, shotgun metagenomics, 16S and full-length rRNA operon) as compared to relationships between metabolomics data and sequence data from eukaryotes (that is, 18S and ITS). There are also strong relationships between difference sequence data from Bacteria and Archaea (rho &gt; 0.2 in bolded font; &gt;0.4 in bolded italics; &gt;0.5 additionally underlined).</p></div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig8"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 3. Microbially-related metabolite and microbial taxon composition among geographic locations for all non-saline soil samples.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig8_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/894b902af2e0/41564_2022_1266_Fig8_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e4940" height="406" width="720" alt="Extended Data Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, Metabolite richness. <strong>b</strong>, Microbe richness. For <strong>a</strong> and <strong>b</strong>, the chi-squared statistic from a Kruskal-Wallis rank sum test for differences in richness across environments is shown (that is, each test had <em>p</em>-value &lt; 2.2 x 10<sup>-16</sup>). <strong>c</strong>, Beta-diversity based on metabolites (upper panel) and microbes (lower panel). Results from PERMANOVA tests (<em>n</em> = 999 permutations) for variance explained by salinity as well as each level of EMPO are shown; <em>p</em>-value = 0.001 for all tests.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig9"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 4. Clustering of samples by environments highlighting beta-diversity based on shotgun metagenomics data for microbial functions.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/56daa61d8aed/41564_2022_1266_Fig9_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e4960" height="425" width="696" alt="Extended Data Fig. 4"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Robust Aitchison PCA with samples colored by EMPO 4 and shaped by salinity. Features are KEGG ECs (that is, enzymes). Results from PERMANOVA tests (<em>n</em> = 999 permutations) for variance explained by salinity as well as each level of EMPO are shown; <em>p</em>-value = 0.001 for all tests.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par16">In the absence of complete turnover in metabolites and microbial taxa across environments, apparent in the overlap of clusters representing different habitats in our ordinations (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c,d</a>), we quantified nestedness. Nestedness describes the degree to which features in one environment are nested subsets of another environment, and can provide insight into community assembly dynamics<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>,<a href="#CR53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR53">53</a></sup>. We found that samples were significantly nested on the basis of both metabolites (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5</a>) and microbial taxa (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">6</a>), and that certain environments were consistently nested within others, although this pattern varied between datasets. For example, on the basis of microbial taxa, we observed host-associated samples to be nested within free-living ones (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">6a</a>); however, the opposite was true for metabolites (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5a</a>). When considering host association and salinity (that is, EMPO 2) for metabolites, free-living samples were more nested than host-associated ones, and within each group, non-saline samples were more nested than saline ones (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5d</a>). This pattern remained consistent when describing metabolites at the superclass, class and molecular formula levels (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5d</a>). Patterns of nestedness were less consistent across taxonomic levels when based on microbial taxa, although non-saline, free-living samples were the most nested across the family, genus and species levels (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">6d</a>). When considering all environments together (that is, for EMPO 3 and 4), we observed stronger patterns of nestedness among environments for microbial taxa (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5b,c</a>) vs metabolites (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">6b,c</a>). However, we observed that patterns of nestedness were somewhat similar between microbial taxa and metabolites for host-associated environments, except for plant surfaces (Extended Data Figs. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">5e</a> and <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">6e</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig10"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 5. Nestedness of community composition based on microbially-related metabolites.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig10_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/34a17de235e3/41564_2022_1266_Fig10_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e5007" height="402" width="722" alt="Extended Data Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, Presence-absence of superclasses across samples, with superclasses (rows) sorted by prevalence and samples (columns, <em>n =</em> 618) sorted by richness. With increasing sample richness, superclasses tended to be gained but not lost (SES = 108.61, <em>p</em>-value &lt; 0.0001 vs. a null model from a two-tailed test; nestedness measure based on overlap and decreasing fills [NODF] statistic = 0.87). Samples are colored by EMPO 2. <strong>b</strong>, As in <strong>a</strong> but with samples colored by EMPO 3. <strong>c</strong>, As in <strong>a</strong> but with samples colored by EMPO 4. <strong>d</strong>, Nestedness as a function of annotation level, from superclass to molecular formula, across all samples and within environments based on EMPO 2. Also shown are median null model NODF scores (± s.d.) for all samples, as well as samples at each level of EMPO 2. NODF measures the average fraction of metabolites from less diverse communities that occur in more diverse communities. All environments at all annotation levels examined were more nested than expected randomly, with nestedness higher at higher annotation levels (<em>p</em>-value &lt; 0.0001 for all comparisons, from two-tailed tests). <strong>e</strong>, As in <strong>c</strong> but with each environment at EMPO 2 shown separately, with samples colored by EMPO 4.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig11"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 6. Nestedness of community composition based on microbial taxa.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig11_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/087f308aa10c/41564_2022_1266_Fig11_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e5052" height="382" width="721" alt="Extended Data Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig11/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Presence-absence of phyla across samples, with phyla (rows) sorted by prevalence and samples (columns, <em>n =</em> 612) sorted by richness. With increasing sample richness, phyla tended to be gained but not lost (SES = 91.86, <em>p</em>-value &lt; 0.0001 vs. a null model; nestedness measure based on overlap and decreasing fills [NODF] statistic = 0.78). Samples are colored by EMPO 2. <strong>b</strong>, As in <strong>a</strong> but with samples colored by EMPO 3. <strong>c</strong>, As in <strong>a</strong> but with samples colored by EMPO 4. <strong>d</strong>, Nestedness as a function of taxonomic level, from phylum to species, across all samples and within environments based on EMPO 2. Also shown are median null model NODF scores (± s.d.) for all samples, as well as samples at each level of EMPO 2. NODF measures the average fraction of taxa from less diverse communities that occur in more diverse communities. All environments at all taxonomic levels examined were more nested than expected randomly, with nestedness higher at higher taxonomic levels (<em>p</em>-value &lt; 0.0001 for all comparisons, from two-tailed tests). <strong>e</strong>, As in <strong>c</strong> but with each environment at EMPO 2 shown separately, with samples colored by EMPO 4.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolites and microbes distinguish habitats</h3>
<p id="Par17">On the basis of the strong relationships among metabolites, microbes and the environment, we next tested the hypothesis that specific metabolites, microbial taxa or microbial functional products (that is, enzymes) could be used to classify samples among environments. Importantly, features useful in classifying samples among habitats can be used as indicators, which can be useful for detecting certain environmental states, environmental change, or in predicting the diversity of other features. Using a machine-learning classifier (see Online Methods), we identified specific metabolites that classified samples among environments with 88.0% overall accuracy (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4a</a>, Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig12" class="usa-link">7a</a>, and Supplementary Fig. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a> and Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">7</a>). After ranking all metabolites on the basis of their impact in distinguishing environments, we found those top ranked to include a diterpenoid negatively associated with non-saline soils (C<sub>20</sub>H<sub>32</sub>, ID: 04492), an undescribed metabolite positively associated with marine sediments (ID: 42202) and a lignan negatively associated with freshwater sediments (C<sub>20</sub>H<sub>20</sub>O<sub>5</sub>, ID: 07899) (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4a</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">7</a>). Among the top 20 ranked metabolites with annotations, the majority were alkaloids, fatty acids or terpenoids, with terpenoids being the most impactful among the top 10 ranked metabolites, including the most highly ranked one (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4a</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4. Machine-learning analysis of microbially related metabolites, microbial taxa and microbial functions, highlighting the top 20 most impactful features for each dataset.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig4_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/ce772fdc0542/41564_2022_1266_Fig4_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e3188" height="559" width="721" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, The top 20 most impactful microbially related metabolites. Features are coloured by metabolite pathway. Metabolites in bold font are those also identified as important in differential abundance analysis (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a>). <strong>b</strong>, The top 20 most impactful microbial taxa (that is, OGUs). Taxa are coloured by phylum. <strong>c</strong>, The top 20 most impactful microbial functions (that is, KEGG ECs). Boxplots are in the style of Tukey, where the centre line indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker is 1.5× IQR from its respective hinge. Enzymes are coloured by class. For all features, ranks are based on impacts derived from SHAP values. Associations with environments are indicated, where + indicates a positive association and – indicates a negative association based on feature abundances. Diamonds and values to the right of boxes indicate means. Values in parentheses indicate (1) the number of iterations (<em>n</em> = 20) in which a feature had no impact and (2) the number of iterations in which the reported association was observed, for cases in which values were &lt;20. Environments are described by the Earth Microbiome Project Ontology (EMPO 4).</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig12"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 7. Machine-learning analysis of microbially-related metabolites, microbial taxa, and microbial functions, highlighting per-environment classification performance.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig12_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/56c7052ba0f1/41564_2022_1266_Fig12_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e5074" height="689" width="721" alt="Extended Data Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig12/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, The F1 score (that is, which considers precision and recall) for each environment as well as overall across all environments. For each data layer, every environment is represented by <em>n</em> = 20 iterations. <strong>b</strong>, Confusion matrices for each data layer highlighting which pairs of environments are confused. Boxplots are in the style of Tukey, where the center line indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first- and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker 1.5 x the interquartile range (IQR) from its respective hinge. For all analyses, environments are described by the Earth Microbiome Project Ontology (EMPO 4).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par18">We also found strong support among methods for the importance of particular metabolites in distinguishing environments. For example, the undescribed metabolite positively associated with marine sediments (that is, ID: 42202) and one fatty acid—a monoacylglycerol (that is, ID: 42202)—revealed as useful in classification in this analysis also stood out in our analysis of differential abundance (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4a</a>, and Supplementary Tables <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a> and <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">7</a>). Similarly, distinct analytical approaches identified specific metabolites as particularly important for distinguishing aquatic samples (that is, one glycerolipid, C<sub>28</sub>H<sub>58</sub>O<sub>15</sub>, ID: 14665 and one pseudoalkaloid, C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>22</sub>N<sub>7</sub>O<sub>5</sub>, ID: 14675), non-saline plant surface samples (that is, one chalcone, C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O, ID: 4949) and non-saline animal distal gut samples (that is, one cholane steroid, C<sub>24</sub>H<sub>38</sub>O<sub>4</sub>, ID: 2552 and one prenyl quinone monoterpenoid, C<sub>29</sub>H<sub>46</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, ID: 22299) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c</a>, and Supplementary Tables <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a> and <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">4</a>).</p>
<p id="Par19">Using the same machine-learning approach on our metagenomic sequence data, we identified specific microbial taxa and microbial functional products (that is, enzymes) useful in classifying samples to environments, with 88.8% and 88.9% overall accuracy, respectively (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4b,c</a>, Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig12" class="usa-link">7a</a>, and Supplementary Figs. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">2</a> and <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">3</a>). We observed that the majority of the top 20 ranked microbial taxa with respect to classification performance were Proteobacteria (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4b</a>). Cyanobacteria, Firmicutes and Actinobacteria were represented by a few members each, and <em>Candidatus</em> Tectomicrobia and Euryarchaeota were represented as singletons (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4b</a>). The most highly ranked taxon, <em>Conexibacter woesei</em> (G000424625, Actinobacteria), was positively associated with non-saline soils, and is an early-diverging member of the class Actinobacteria first isolated from temperate forest soil in Italy<sup><a href="#CR54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR54">54</a></sup> (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4b</a>). Also among the top ranked taxa were <em>Haloquadratum walsbyi</em> (Euryarchaeota) positively associated with saline soils, and <em>Pantoea dispersa</em> (<em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>) positively associated with the detritus of terrestrial plants (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4b</a>). For microbial functions, we note that the majority of the top 20 most highly ranked enzymes with respect to classification performance were oxidoreductases or transferases, followed by hydrolases, and then isomerases and lyases (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4c</a>). The most highly ranked enzyme was positively associated with non-saline soils and was a trehalohydrase (enzyme code (EC): 3.2.1.141), an enzyme that binds trehalose, a carbon-source commonly produced by soil inhabitants including plants, invertebrates, bacteria and fungi, with potential roles in symbioses<sup><a href="#CR55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR55">55</a></sup>. Also among the most highly ranked enzymes were a glutamate carboxylase (EC: 4.1.1.90) positively associated with the surfaces of marine plants, and a linoleate lipoxygenase (EC: 1.13.11.60) positively associated with lichen thalli (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4c</a>).</p></section><section id="Sec8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolite–microbe co-occurrences are habitat-specific</h3>
<p id="Par20">In addition to exploring relationships between metabolite and microbial diversity, we sought to explicitly quantify metabolite–microbe co-occurrence patterns. Beyond relating metabolites to the microbes that potentially interact with them, certain metabolite–microbe pairs may have stronger associations with the environment than any one feature set alone and may serve as emergent indicators. In particular, we examined associations between metabolites and the environment (for example, Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a,c</a>) while also considering each metabolite’s co-occurrence with all microbes in the dataset (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">1</a>). In that regard, we first generated metabolite–microbe co-occurrences learned from both LC–MS/MS- and shotgun metagenomic profiles across all samples, for a cross-section of 6,501 microbially related metabolites and 4,120 microbial taxa (Extended Data Figs. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">8</a> and 9). Whereas most metabolites co-occurred with at least a few microbes, few metabolites were found to co-occur with many microbes (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">8a</a>). The distribution of co-occurrences was not heavily shifted towards any particular pathway (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">8b</a>); however, certain superclasses exhibited co-occurrences with many microbes, including diarylheptanoids and phenylethanoids (C6-C3) (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">8c</a>). Similarly for microbes, co-occurrences with metabolites were not heavily skewed towards particular phyla, although specific clades were enriched, such as the most recently diverged members of the Bacteroidetes (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">9</a>). In contrast to their co-occurrences with metabolites, changes in microbial abundances with respect to the environment appear to be phylogenetically conserved, and correlated with salinity and association with the animal gut environment (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">9</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig13"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 8. Summary of co-occurrence ranks for microbially-related metabolites.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig13_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/1497dac381c5/41564_2022_1266_Fig13_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e5118" height="511" width="722" alt="Extended Data Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>a</strong>, Distribution of the percentage of microbial taxa for which co-occurrences were strong. Strong co-occurrence was defined as having a co-occurrence strength (that is, rank, or log conditional probability) ≥ 2. The overall distribution of co-occurrence strengths is shown in the inset (<em>n</em> = 26,784,120). For values &gt; 0 (<em>n</em> = 13,851,755), the minimum = –10.17, maximum = 12.69, mean = 2.40 x 10<sup>-18</sup>, median = 0.08, and mode = 1.22. For values ≥ 2 (<em>n</em> = 3,496,639), the minimum = 2.00, maximum = 12.69, mean = 2.87, median = 2.63, and mode = 4.26. <strong>b</strong>, The percentage of microbial taxa for which co-occurrences were strong (that is, ≥ 2), across metabolite pathways. <strong>c</strong>, The percentage of microbial taxa for which co-occurrences were strong (that is, ≥ 2), across metabolite superclasses. For panels <strong>b</strong> and <strong>c</strong>, points were jittered horizontally for clarity, and <em>n</em> = 4,765 metabolites. Boxplots are in the style of Tukey, where the center line indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first- and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker 1.5 x the interquartile range (IQR) from its respective hinge.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig14"><h4 class="obj_head">Extended Data Fig. 9. Phylogenetic relationships among microbial taxa highlighting log fold changes in abundance relative to environment, and overall co-occurrences with microbially-related metabolites.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9712116_41564_2022_1266_Fig14_ESM.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ae81/9712116/689b8986a25d/41564_2022_1266_Fig14_ESM.jpg" loading="lazy" id="d32e5141" height="423" width="721" alt="Extended Data Fig. 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig14/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Branches are colored by microbial phylum. Annotations include Domain and Phylum level associations (and Class for <em>Proteobacteria</em>), heat maps representing log fold changes in relative abundance for each environment (from <em>songbird</em>), and heat maps summarizing co-occurrences with microbially-related metabolites (from <em>mmvec</em>). Co-occurrence strength indicates (1) the percentage of all microbially-related metabolites for which the co-occurrence rank (that is, log conditional probability) was ≥ 2 (that is, strong), and (2) the median co-occurrence rank value, considering only strong values (in parentheses in the legend).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par21">Next, using metabolite–metabolite distances based on co-occurrence profiles considering all microbes, we ordinated metabolites in microbe space. We then examined correlations between metabolite loadings on the principal coordinates of that co-occurrence ordination and (1) log fold changes of metabolites across environments (for example, Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3a</a>) and (2) distributions of metabolites across all samples (that is, loadings and overall magnitude from ordination of all samples) (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c</a>), and found strong relationships with each (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5a</a>). In particular, the abundances of microbially related metabolites in plant surface (saline), sediment (saline) and aquatic samples (that is, those from water) had strong correlations with microbe–metabolite co-occurrences (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5a</a>). Focusing on seawater (that is, Water (saline)), we visualized the correlation between metabolite loadings on PC1 of the co-occurrence ordination, which represent differences based on co-occurrences with microbes (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5b</a>), and log fold changes in metabolite abundances with respect to seawater (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5c</a>). In this space, features with high values for both vectors should be associated with the same microbes and also highly abundant in the ocean, whereas features with low values for both vectors should be associated with the same microbes and have low-to-zero abundance in the ocean (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5c</a>). Focusing on one group of carbohydrates (excluding glycosides) and one group of terpenoids (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5c,d</a>), we found significant differences in their intensities in seawater vs all other environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5e</a>), as well as in the abundances of their top co-occurring microbial taxa (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5f</a>). Importantly, by relying on our metabolite intensity data, this result validates patterns identified in our analyses of differential abundance across environments and co-occurrence with microbial taxa. We used this same approach to explore metabolite–microbe co-occurrences specific to other environments (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">10</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">5</a>), further revealing strong turnover in metabolite–microbe co-occurrences across habitats.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5. Metabolite–microbe co-occurrences vary across environments.</h4>

For panels <strong>c</strong>, <strong>d</strong>, <strong>g</strong>, <strong>h</strong>, <strong>k</strong>, and <strong>l</strong>, points represent samples, and results from a two-sided <em>t</em>-test comparing the focal vs. all other environments are shown. Boxplots are Tukey’s, where the center indicates the median, lower and upper hinges the first- and third quartiles, respectively, and each whisker 1.5 x the interquartile range (IQR) from its hinge.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Correlations with amplicon sequence data and GC–MS data</h3>
<p id="Par22">To begin to explore the additional data generated for EMP500 samples, including GC–MS and amplicon sequence data (that is, bacterial and archaeal 16S and full-length rRNA operon, eukaryotic 18S, fungal ITS), we compared sample–sample distances (that is, beta-diversity) between each pair of datasets. Beyond providing insight into how certain community data are related, strong correlations between datasets may indicate similarity in the structuring of features among samples or habitats. Importantly, we found further support for a strong relationship between microbially related metabolites and microbial taxa (LC–MS/MS vs 16S; <em>r</em> = 0.27, <em>P</em> = 0.001) (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). The relationships between the metabolomics data (that is, LC–MS/MS or GC–MS) and sequence data from eukaryotes (that is, 18S or ITS) were weaker (for example, LC–MS/MS vs ITS; <em>r</em> = 0.07, <em>P</em> = 0.006) (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). The weakest relationships were between sequence data from Bacteria and Archaea (that is, 16S or shotgun metagenomics) and sequence data from eukaryotes (that is, 18S or ITS) (for example, shotgun metagenomics for taxa vs. 18S; <em>r</em> = –0.002, <em>P</em> = 0.9) (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). The strongest relationships were between different layers of sequence data from Bacteria and Archaea (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). For example, correlations between 16S rRNA profiles and those from full-length rRNA operons had <em>r</em> = 0.55 (<em>P</em> = 0.001), and 16S vs shotgun metagenomics (taxa) had <em>r</em> = 0.51 (<em>P</em> = 0.001) (Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>). These results highlight the strong relationship between metabolic profiles and microbial taxonomic composition across habitats spanning the globe.</p></section></section><section id="Sec10"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p id="Par23">Here we discuss some of the caveats and limitations of our study, and further highlight how our approach advances understanding of microbial community dynamics and functional diversity. Due to their extensive nature, we provide additional important points of discussion as <a href="#FPar2" class="usa-link">Supplementary Information</a>. We begin by recognizing that certain environments included in EMPO are represented here by only a handful of samples (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a>) and/or a single sample set (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>), and note that we had to exclude them from some of our analyses due to low representation (for example, machine learning and co-occurrence analyses). We recommend that future efforts focus on additional sampling of these environments to further generalize our findings to those habitats. Similarly, we hope to expand sampling geographically to broaden our scope of inference, as many important environments and locations could not be included here (or, indeed, in the EMP’s 27,000-sample dataset<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>). We also note that the inherent design of the EMP (that is, crowd-sourced samples from experts in respective fields) prevented us from explicitly exploring causation with respect to the environment in our analysis, and thus our findings are based largely on observations and correlations among feature sets and associated metadata.</p>
<p id="Par24">In our example analysis, we explored whether every metabolite is everywhere but the environment selects (that is, the Baas Becking hypothesis<sup><a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">42</a>,<a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">43</a></sup>, but for microbially related metabolites). Whereas we interpret our findings as strong evidence that every metabolite is everywhere but the environment selects, our study was not designed to address this hypothesis explicitly, and further evidence is needed to support this hypothesis. For example, features at abundances below the detection limit of our approach could not be considered here, but may alter our view of these patterns. Similarly, although input sample volumes were normalized as best as possible, they may influence estimates of alpha-diversity, and the values reported here probably exhibit some error in part due to this influence. We also identified metabolite–microbe co-occurrences, and note that our approach for characterizing co-occurrences, ‘mmvec’<sup><a href="#CR56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR56">56</a></sup>, does not currently allow for controlling for covariates and this may influence results. However, in our analysis we were able to include EMPO as a variable, which we designed to account for variation among environments that may not be captured by available metadata.</p>
<p id="Par25">Here we described patterns of turnover, nestedness and co-occurrence of metabolites and microbes across a diverse set of environments while addressing ecological questions surrounding the distribution of metabolites and their relationships with microbial taxonomic and functional diversity. One outstanding question in microbial ecology asks how microbial taxon profiles can be integrated with functional ones<sup><a href="#CR57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR57">57</a></sup>. Here, in addition to describing microbial taxa, their functions and their metabolites, we explicitly tested for metabolite–microbe co-occurrences and explored how they relate to the environment, for which we have outlined our approach (Extended Data Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">1</a>). Our analysis provides insight into biological processes including microbial community assembly and links microbial taxonomic profiles with metabolism and functional diversity (that is, enzymes) at planetary scale. Our work provides an initial view of how microbially related metabolites are structured with respect to factors including host association, salinity and the presence of certain microbes (Figs. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a> and <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5</a>). Importantly, we identified the most abundant and highly ranked pathway representing the metabolites best able to distinguish environments to be terpenoids<sup><a href="#CR58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR58">58</a></sup>, highlighting the importance of this group of metabolites in distinguishing Earth’s environments (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4a</a> and Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">7</a>).</p>
<p id="Par26">We acknowledge that previous studies describing microbial taxa and function using globally distributed sample sets, such as for the human gut, soils and the ocean, have shown that both can vary across locations<sup><a href="#CR59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR59">59</a>–<a href="#CR62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR62">62</a></sup>. Similarly, studies examining metabolite profiles across changes in microbial community composition, or environmental stress such as from heat, have shown variation associated with either<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>,<a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">21</a></sup> or both<sup><a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a></sup>. Furthermore, among previous multi-omics studies combining metagenomics with metatranscriptomics, metaproteomics and/or metabolomics, some of which have shown the correlation between data layers to vary across sites, the majority are focused on a single environment<sup><a href="#CR63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR63">63</a>–<a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">73</a></sup>. Here we performed multi-omics integration of a dataset encompassing a diversity of environmental sample types representing several habitats, generated using standardized methods allowing for robust meta-analysis with data from other studies using the same approach.</p>
<p id="Par27">Our approach illustrates that recent advances in computational annotation tools offer a powerful toolbox to interpret untargeted metabolomics data<sup><a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a></sup>. We anticipate that parallel advances in metagenomic sequencing, genome assembly and genome mining will improve the discovery and classification of functional products from among microbes and provide additional insight into these findings. By following standardized methods available on GitHub and making this dataset publicly available in Qiita and GNPS, this study will serve as an important resource for continued collaborative investigations. In the same manner, the development of optimized instrumentation and computational methods for metabolomics will expand the depth of metabolites surveyed in microbiome studies.</p></section><section id="Sec11"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Dataset description</h3>
<section id="Sec13"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Sample collection</h4>
<p id="Par28">Our research complies with all relevant ethical regulations following policies at the University of California, San Diego (UCSD). Animal samples that were sequenced were not collected at UCSD and are not for vertebrate animals research at UCSD following the UCSD Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC). Samples were contributed by 34 principal investigators of the Earth Microbiome Project 500 (EMP500) Consortium and are samples from studies at their respective institutions (Supplementary Table <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>). Relevant permits and ethics information for each parent study are described in the ‘Permits for sample collection’ section below. Samples were contributed as distinct sets referred to here as studies, where each study represented a single environment (for example, terrestrial plant detritus). To achieve more even coverage across microbial environments, we devised an ontology of sample types (microbial environments), the EMP Ontology (EMPO) (<a href="http://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/empo/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://earthmicrobiome.org/protocols-and-standards/empo/</a>)<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>, and selected samples to fill out EMPO categories as broadly as possible. EMPO recognizes strong gradients structuring microbial communities globally, and thus classifies microbial environments (level 4) on the basis of host association (level 1), salinity (level 2), host kingdom (if host-associated) or phase (if free-living) (level 3) (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1a</a>). As we anticipated previously<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>, we have updated the number of levels as well as states therein for EMPO (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1b</a>) on the basis of an important additional salinity gradient observed among host-associated samples when considering the previously unreported shotgun metagenomic and metabolomic data generated here (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3c,d</a>). We note that although we were able to acquire samples for all EMPO categories, some categories are represented by a single study.</p>
<p id="Par29">Samples were collected following the Earth Microbiome Project sample submission guide<sup><a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">50</a></sup>. Briefly, samples were collected fresh, split into 10 aliquots and then frozen, or alternatively collected and frozen, and subsequently split into 10 aliquots with minimal perturbation. Aliquot size was sufficient to yield 10–100 ng genomic DNA (approximately 10<sup>7</sup>–10<sup>8</sup> cells). To leave samples amenable to chemical characterization (metabolomics), buffers or solutions for sample preservation (for example, RNAlater) were avoided. Ethanol (50–95%) was allowed as it is compatible with LC–MS/MS although it should also be avoided if possible.</p>
<p id="Par30">Sampling guidance was tailored for four general sample types: bulk unaltered (for example, soil, sediment, faeces), bulk fractionated (for example, sponges, corals, turbid water), swabs (for example, biofilms) and filters. Bulk unaltered samples were split fresh (or frozen), sampled into 10 pre-labelled 2 ml screw-cap bead beater tubes (Sarstedt, 72.694.005 or similar), ideally with at least 200 mg biomass, and flash frozen in liquid nitrogen (if possible). Bulk fractionated samples were fractionated as appropriate for the sample type, split into 10 pre-labelled 2 ml screw-cap bead beater tubes, ideally with at least 200 mg biomass, and flash frozen in liquid nitrogen (if possible). Swabs were collected as 10 replicate swabs using 5 BD SWUBE dual cotton swabs with wooden stick and screw cap (281130). Filters were collected as 10 replicate filters (47 mm diameter, 0.2 um pore size, polyethersulfone (preferred) or hydrophilic PTFE filters), placed in pre-labelled 2 ml screw-cap bead beater tubes, and flash frozen in liquid nitrogen (if possible). All sample types were stored at –80 °C if possible, otherwise –20 °C.</p>
<p id="Par31">To track the provenance of sample aliquots, we employed a QR coding scheme. Labels were affixed to aliquot tubes before shipping when possible. QR codes had the format ‘name.99.s003.a05’, where ‘name’ is the PI name, ‘99’ is the study ID, ‘s003’ is the sample number and ‘a05’ is the aliquot number. QR codes (version 2, 25 pixels × 25 pixels) were printed on 1.125’ × 0.75’ rectangular and 0.437’ circular cap Cryogenic Direct Thermal labels (GA International, DFP-70) using a Zebra model GK420d printer and ZebraDesigner Pro 3 software for Windows. After receipt but before aliquots were stored in freezers, QR codes were scanned into a sample inventory spreadsheet using a QR scanner.</p></section><section id="Sec14"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Sample metadata</h4>
